  agent step 1: content: Ligand 2 is the high-QED azetidine anilide I built earlier; ligand 1 is the benzyl urea anilinopyrimidine. The pattern I have established is that jnk3 rises with the count of aryl-NH-(hetero)aryl linkages: one link gives about 0.07, two give 0.08-0.10. The untested question is whether a third link keeps paying, and ligand 1 is one edit away from answering it. It currently has two usable links (the urea NH on the anilide side and the pyrimidin-2-ylamino NH); its third NH is wasted because it connects to a CH2, not to a ring. Atom 1 is the urea carbonyl and atom 2 is that CH2-bearing NH, a non-ring bond, so cutting there discards the NH, the CH2 and the phenyl together. It also costs almost nothing in mass (~306 Da) and pulls LogP from 3.54 down near 2.2, so QED should hold up better than the 413 Da route ligand 2 came from.

  agent step 1: content: I have established that jnk3 rises with the number of aryl-NH-(hetero)aryl linkages, but not whether what matters is the N-H donor itself or merely the nitrogen bridge joining two rings. That distinction decides every future design: if the donor is essential I must protect those NH groups, and if it is not, I can methylate them and gain QED for free, because removing a donor and lowering TPSA is exactly what QED rewards. What I have learned about this target is specific: the active element is an aryl-N(H)-azine link with a free donor hydrogen - methylating that NH takes activity to exactly zero - and the azine must stay monocyclic, since fusing a benzo ring onto it (quinazoline, benzothiazole) collapses jnk3 to 0.01-0.05.
